Multimodal data integration and cross-modal querying via orchestrated approximate message passing
Sagnik Nandy, Zongming Ma

Abstract
The need for multimodal data integration arises naturally when multiple complementary sets of features are measured on the same sample, such as in single-cell multi-omics studies. Under a dependent multifactor model, we develop a fully data-driven orchestrated approximate message passing algorithm for integrating information across these feature sets to achieve statistically optimal signal recovery. In practice, these reference datasets are often queried later by new subjects that are only partially observed. Leveraging on asymptotic normality of estimates generated by our data integration method, we further develop an asymptotically valid prediction set for the latent factor representation of any such query subject. We demonstrate the prowess of both the data integration and the prediction set construction algorithms synthetic examples and real world single-cell datasets. Keywords: Empirical Bayes, Factor analysis, Prediction set, Principal component analysis, Single-cell, Multi-omics.



1
Introduction

1.1
Motivation

Identifying and documenting different cell populations and cell states from single-cell RNA sequencing (scRNAseq) data is an important problem in cell biology
[
41
,
11
]
.
Funding agencies have organized consortium level efforts to build large scale reference datasets (a.k.a. atlases) for different tissues and organs in different biological conditions.
Such efforts include but are not limited to the Human Biomolecular Atlas Project (HuBMAP)
[
50
]
, the Human Tumor Atlas Network (HTAN)
[
47
]
, the Molecular Atlas of Lung Development Program (LungMAP)
[
18
]
, etc.
They have resulted in high-quality expert-annotated reference datasets or atlases that can be later used for classifying and annotating new cells of similar types.
The practice is known as “label transfer”.
Though scRNAseq can help separate heterogeneous groups of cells, transcriptomics alone often is unable to separate molecularly similar but functionally different types of cells.
To resolve this issue, biologists often rely on other molecular or structural features observed along with scRNAseq data.

Multimodal technologies that measure different single-cell level characteristics have gained tremendous popularity in recent years
[
54
]
.
These technologies allow for the availability of transcriptome data along with other single-cell level biochemical properties, such as surface protein abundance
[
52
]
and chromatin accessibility
[
10
,
12
]
, among others. With the availability of multimodal data, it becomes important to design efficient data integration techniques to combine information from these heterogenous modalities to create a unified low-dimensional embedding to segregate cells having molecular and functional dissimilarities.

In practical settings, multimodal reference atlases are frequently queried by new cells measured in a single modality, or even a subset of features within one modality.
This task, often referred to as
label transfer
, involves inferring cell types of new observations by
mapping them to the embedding space learned from reference data.
While conceptually related to supervised learning, label transfer differs in that it often requires cross-modality mapping and domain adaptation, as the new data may not share the same feature space or modality as the reference atlas. These challenges make it essential not only to produce accurate predictions of cell identity, but also to quantify the uncertainty associated with each prediction, particularly when query data are noisy, incomplete, or potentially unmatched to known cell types in the reference.

In
[
23
]
, the authors developed a weighted-nearest-neighbor (WNN) approach for integrating multimodal data.
Their method can handle multiple datasets with different dimensions and is state-of-the-art in the literature to efficiently segregate different cells into interpretable categories based on the structural and molecular similarities inferred from
the multimodal single-cell observations.
Other popular methods include CiteFuse
[
28
]
, MOFA+
[
1
]
, and totalVI
[
19
]
.
However, these methods suffer from a common shortcoming from
a
statistical perspective:
They are built on heuristics and lack solid mathematical foundations that enable theoretically justifiable generalization beyond the data examples shown in the original papers in which they were proposed.
Moreover, to the best of our knowledge, none of these methods generates an atlas that allows uncertainty quantification of the predicted cell state when queried.

To address these issues, we propose in this paper a new multimodal data integration method that generates atlases with provable performance guarantees under a stylized model and provides an asymptotically valid prediction set for any desired coverage probability when the constructed atlas is queried by a new subject with partial observation of the modalities and the features used in atlas construction.

Although we have motivated the problem studied by this manuscript mainly with single-cell multi-omics data analysis, the model we shall propose is general and so are the developed methods and the related theory.

1.2
Problem formulation

In general, one can view multimodal data as multiple datasets with correspondence of measured subjects.
If each dataset is stored as a subject-by-feature matrix, then these data matrices have corresponding rows.

We model

the latent state encodings of the same subject across different datasets as
dependent

signal

vectors and design a multi-orbit approximate message passing algorithm in each iteration of which we synchronously integrate information across different datasets to denoise each estimate of the latent space representation vector specific to an individual dataset.
The resulting
algorithm

has a number of desirable characteristics: fast computation, provable statistical optimality under reasonable modeling assumptions, and the availability of asymptotically valid confidence statements.

We develop our method based on the following dependent multimodal factor model.
For any positive integer
\ell
, let
[\ell]=\{1,\dots,\ell\}
be the count set.
Suppose all available datasets come in the form of
m
high-dimensional and
\widetilde{m}
low-dimensional datasets,
where
m\geq 1
,
\widetilde{m}\geq 0
, and
m+\widetilde{m}\geq 2
.
Suppose
for each
h\in[m]
, the high-dimensional data matrix

\bm{X}_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times p_{h}}

satisfies

\bm{X}_{h}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\bm{U}_{h}\bm{D}_{h}\bm{V}_{h}^{\top}+\bm{W}_{h},

(1.1)

where
\bm{U}_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times r_{h}}
,
\bm{V}_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{h}\times r_{h}}
,
\bm{D}_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{h}\times r_{h}}
and
\bm{W}_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times p_{h}}
.
Hence, each
\bm{X}_{h}
can be viewed as a noisy observation of the low-rank signal
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\bm{U}_{h}\bm{D}_{h}\bm{V}_{h}^{\top}
representing effective information present in the matrix.

We assume that each
\bm{D}_{h}
is diagonal with positive diagonal entries,
{\bm{U}}_{h}^{\top}{\bm{U}}_{h}\approx N{\bm{I}}_{r_{h}}
, and
{\bm{V}}_{h}^{\top}{\bm{V}}_{h}\approx p_{h}{\bm{I}}_{h}
.
In other words, up to scaling, each
\bm{U}_{h}\bm{D}_{h}\bm{V}_{h}^{\top}
is essentially the singular value decomposition of the signal component.
We do not enforce strict orthonormalization of
{\bm{U}}_{h}
’s and
{\bm{V}}_{h}
’s as we shall assume that their rows are sampled from certain priors and we shall make this assumption precise below.

In addition,
assume each low-dimensional matrix
\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times{\widetilde{r}}_{\ell}}

satisfies

\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\ell}=\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{\ell}\bm{L}^{\top}_{\ell}+\widetilde{\bm{W}}_{\ell},

(1.2)

where
\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times{\widetilde{r}}_{\ell}}
and
\bm{L}_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\widetilde{r}}_{\ell}\times{\widetilde{r}}_{\ell}}
. We assume that for all
\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]
, the matrices
\bm{L}_{\ell}
are symmetric and full-rank.
The low dimensional matrix
\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\ell}
can be viewed as a noisy observation of the low-dimensional signal
\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{\ell}\bm{L}^{\top}_{\ell}
for
\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]
.

Similarly, we assume
\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{\ell}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{\ell}\approx N\bm{I}_{\widetilde{r}_{\ell}}
as the rows of
\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{\ell}
’s are also sampled from prior.

Throughout the paper, we differentiate high- and low-dimensional datasets in asymptotic sense:
for high-dimensional datasets, we assume proportional growth asymptotics where
p_{h}/N\rightarrow\gamma_{h}\in(0,\infty)

and
r_{h}=O(1)
is fixed
as both
N
and
p_{h}\rightarrow\infty
for
h\in[m]
, and
for low-dimensional datasets, we assume that
{\widetilde{r}}_{\ell}=O(1)
is fixed for all
\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]
as
N\rightarrow\infty
.

The assumption of a low-rank latent structure for the signal component of the high-dimensional modalities is widely used in both empirical and theoretical works across several disciplines. In single-cell biology, it is widely accepted that a relatively small number of biological programs, such as differentiation trajectories and cell-cycle phases,
drive the observed high-dimensional variation in gene
expressions. This motivates the modeling of each modality as arising from a low-dimensional latent representation, a framework that has been adopted in numerous recent works including
[
23
,
19
,
1
,
58
,
13
]
.
Beyond biology, similar assumptions have been adopted in machine learning and artificial intelligence, where it is often assumed that high-dimensional observations lie near a low-dimensional manifold.
In the multimodal setting, the different data modalities are typically assumed to be governed by a set of dependent low-dimensional latent factors
[
33
,
43
,
22
]

which capture coordinated structure across modalities, making low-rank modeling a natural and interpretable choice.

The additional low-dimensional modalities in equation (
1.2
) are motivated by an experimental design in single-cell genomics where a
targeted panel
of biomarkers is used.
Such a panel involves a curated set of pre-selected genes or proteins (typically tens
of features) that are known to be biologically informative, and
are often collected alongside more comprehensive but sparse or noisy measurements of the full transcriptome or the full epigenome.

In data integration and atlas construction,
our goal is to
jointly
recover the low-rank signals in (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) from the collection of matrices
\{\bm{X}_{h}:h\in[m]\}
and
\{\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\ell}:\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]\}
.
For the
i
th subject, we regard the
i
th rows in the subject effect matrices
\bm{U}_{h}
for
h\in[m]
and
\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{\ell}
for
\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]
as latent state encodings of this subject provided by different data modalities.
Since the subject is the same and the modalities are different, these encoding vectors are expected to be distinct while dependent.
In particular,
we assume that
\{((\bm{U}_{1})_{i*},\dots,(\bm{U}_{m})_{i*},({\widetilde{\bm{U}}}_{1})_{i*},\dots,({\widetilde{\bm{U}}}_{\widetilde{m}})_{i*}):i\in[N]\}
are i.i.d. samples from a joint prior distribution
\mu
(
supported on a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{\sum_{i=1}^{m}r_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}\widetilde{r}_{j}}
),
through which we are allowed to incorporate any dependence structure among different modalities.
The
dependence of the modality-specific latent state encoding vectors, and hence the signals, necessitates information pooling for their reconstruction.
For other components in (
1.1
)-(
1.2
), we assume that the rows
of each modality effect matrix
\bm{V}_{h}
are sampled independently from some prior
\nu_{h}
and they are mutually independent across
h\in[m]
.
The matrices
\{\bm{D}_{h}:h\in[m]\}
are assumed to be diagonal matrices with positive diagonal entries representing signal strengths characterizing the association between subject effects and modality effects. Furthermore, to ensure identifiability of the columns in
\bm{U}_{h}
and
\bm{V}_{h}
for each
h\in[m]
, we assume that the entries
\{(\bm{D}_{h})_{ii}:i\in[r_{h}]\}
are distinct for all
h\in[m]
.

This assumption is not necessary for the validity of the OrchAMP algorithm or its associated state evolution equations, but is imposed to guarantee identifiability of the singular vectors and to facilitate prediction set construction for query subjects.

The entries of the error matrices
\bm{W}_{h}
for
h\in[m]
and
\widetilde{\bm{W}}_{\ell}
for
\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]
are i.i.d.
N(0,1)
random variables.

In atlas querying and prediction set construction, we assume that we observe, on a query subject, subsets of features on a subset of modalities seen in the reference datasets used for atlas construction.
To be precise, we observe
\{Q_{h_{1}},\ldots,Q_{h_{d}},\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}}\}
on a query subject, where the vectors
Q_{h_{1}},\ldots,Q_{h_{d}}
are from high-dimensional modalities
\{h_{1},\ldots,h_{d}\}\subseteq[m]
and
\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}}
low-dimensional modalities
\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{\widetilde{d}}\}\subseteq[\widetilde{m}]
.
For the
k
-th observed high-dimensional modality,

Q_{h_{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{F}_{h_{k}}|}

where
\mathcal{F}_{h_{k}}\subseteq[p_{h_{k}}]
collects indices of the observed features in this modality.
For the
k
-th observed low-dimensional modality,

\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell_{k}}|}

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell_{k}}\subseteq[\widetilde{r}_{\ell_{k}}]
denotes indices of the observed features.
The observed information on query is assumed to be partial observation of an independent realization of the rows in model (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) with the same
\{\bm{V}_{h},\bm{D}_{h}:h\in[m]\}
,
\{\bm{L}_{\ell}:\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]\}
, and the same latent factor prior distribution
\mu
as in the reference data.
Here, our goal is to leverage both the atlas and the partial observation on query to construct a prediction set for the query’s full latent representation
({U}_{1}^{\mathrm{Q}},\dots,{U}_{m}^{\mathrm{Q}},\widetilde{U}_{1}^{\mathrm{Q}},\dots,\widetilde{U}_{\widetilde{m}}^{\mathrm{Q}})
.

1.2.1
Main contributions

The main contributions of this work include the following:

1.

Proposal of the OrchAMP algorithm for data integration and its theoretical justification
.
We propose and justify a fully data-driven algorithm for the integrative estimation of signal components in model (
1.1
)–(
1.2
).
We establish the “large
N
” asymptotic normality of all iterates of the algorithm under general conditions for a wide range of priors on the signal.
Under an additional unique-fixed-point condition on the approximate message passing state evolution, we further establish the “large
N
, large
t
” asymptotic Bayes optimality of the estimators produced by the algorithm.

2.

Design of an asymptotically valid prediction set for query.
Leveraging on the asymptotic normality of the iterates in the OrchAMP algorithm, we construct asymptotically valid prediction sets for query data in which only a subset of modalities and/or subsets of features within modalities are observed.
The prediction set is valid even when the unique-fixed-point condition on state evolution is not satisfied. Hence, it is widely applicable.
To the best of our knowledge, the present work is the first to construct asymptotically valid prediction sets for queries.

3.

Validation on a single-cell data example.

We validate the proposed atlas building and prediction set construction algorithms on a tri-modal TEA-seq dataset
[
53
]
.
While both algorithms have been motivated by and theoretically validated under model (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) in the first place, we demonstrate on this real dataset that the OrchAMP algorithm achieves comparable state-of-the-art cell state differentiation capacity to its competitors such as WNN, and that the prediction sets constructed by our algorithm are highly informative when we query the constructed atlas with a new cell measured in a single modality.

A demonstration of the proposed methods on the single-cell data example is available at
https://github.com/Sagnik-Nandy/OrchAMP
.

1.3
Related works

Approximate message passing (AMP) was developed by
Donoho et al., [16]
in the context of compressed sensing. Related ideas were also explored in the context of belief propagation in graphical models
[
42
]
and statistical physics
[
36
,
37
]
.
Furthermore, the idea of state evolution constituting a major component of the message-passing algorithms leading to the development of AMP was numerically studied in
[
15
,
48
,
16
]
, among others.

The foundational work of
Bayati and Montanari, [5]
developed a rigorous framework for analyzing the approximate message passing (AMP) algorithm in the context of high-dimensional linear regression, including the LASSO,
and
Javanmard and Montanari, [26]
adapted this framework to study AMP for low-rank matrix recovery problems.
They showed
that AMP can achieve asymptotically Bayes-optimal recovery of low-rank signals when an appropriate class of denoising functions is employed within the iterative updates.
See also
[
7
,
14
]
.
In parallel,
Lesieur et al., [30]
studied constrained low-rank matrix estimation using AMP, precisely characterizing phase transitions in recovery performance under structural priors and non-Gaussian noise, thereby broadening AMP’s applicability beyond classical spiked models.
See also
[
17
,
31
,
39
,
59
]
.

In particular,
Zhong et al., [59]
proposed an empirical Bayes technique to estimate the underlying prior of the signal components and hence established a completely data-driven method to compute the denoising functions in AMP for low-rank matrix recovery in the single-view setting. The present manuscript generalizes this empirical Bayes idea to estimation in multi-view settings and to prediction set construction. The latter is beyond the scope of
[
59
]
even in single-view settings.

The use of AMP in information retrieval with multi-view data, where more than one data sources (views) contain independent pieces of information about the same underlying signal of interest, has been considered in the Statistics/Machine Learning literature.
When one data source is high-dimensional and the other is low-dimensional, researchers have designed specific AMP algorithms around the high-dimensional data source by treating low-dimensional data as side information
[
32
,
4
]
.

When there are multiple high-dimensional sources,
Ma and Nandy, [34]
studied a special case where the signal of interest is a vector (hence of rank one) that is identical across sources and whose entries are i.i.d. random numbers from a (known) Rademacher
prior in a stylized contextual multi-layer two-block network model.
They proposed an elementary form of OrchAMP with multi-orbit message passing to ensure
asymptotically Bayes optimal reconstruction of the signal.
The present manuscript develops this idea from identical rank-one signal to dependent signals of unequal ranks, from an oracle initialization to spectral, from known Rademacher prior to generic unknown priors, and from only low-rank signal recovery to both signal recovery and cross-modal prediction set construction.

See
[
40
]

for the generalization to high-dimensional regression with network side information.

Gerbelot and Berthier, [20]
proposed a unified
perspective

of these AMP algorithms as message passing on edges of an oriented graph whose vertices contain different related modalities.
When preparing this manuscript, we became aware of the recent work by
Keup and Zdeborová, [27]
, which considered a related multimodal set-up consisting of two high-dimensional rank-one spiked random matrices with correlated factors.
They linearized the belief propagation updates to obtain a version of AMP and used it to reconstruct the latent factors from the data matrices.
Furthermore, they quantified the gain in the minimum signal-to-noise ratio required for weak recovery of the correlated factors by integrating the two data matrices compared to that from using a single matrix.
In comparison,
we tackle here the more challenging and realistic scenario where the underlying signals to be recovered from multiple data sources are correlated/dependent rather than identical and have potentially different ranks, the signal priors are unknown, and there exist both high-dimensional and low-dimensional information sources.

1.4
Notations

The notation
\mathbb{R}^{k}
denotes the set of
k
dimensional vectors with real co-ordinates.
For a vector
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, the Euclidean norm is denoted by
\|a\|
and the
p
-th norm for
p\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{2\}
is denoted by
\|a\|_{p}
.
The notation
\mathbb{R}^{k\times\ell}
denotes the set of
k\times\ell
matrices with real entries.
For any set
\mathcal{F}
,
|\mathcal{F}|
denotes its cardinality.
All the matrices will be denoted by upper case bold letters throughout the text.
For any matrix
\bm{A}
,
\bm{A}_{i*}
denotes its
i
-th row and
\bm{A}_{*i}
its
i
-th column.
Furthermore,
\bm{A}_{ij}
denotes its
(i,j)
-th entry.
For any subset
\mathcal{F}
of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
, the submatrix formed by the rows with indices in
\mathcal{F}
is denoted by
\bm{A}_{\mathcal{F},*}
and the submatrix formed by the columns with indices in
\mathcal{F}
is denoted by
\bm{A}_{*,\mathcal{F}}
.
Next, for
\bm{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
,
\bm{A}^{\top}
denotes its transpose and
\mathrm{Tr}(\bm{A})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\bm{A}_{ii}
denotes its trace.
The Frobenius norm of a matrix
\bm{A}
is denoted by
\|\bm{A}\|_{F}

and the spectral norm of
\bm{A}
is denoted by
\|\bm{A}\|
.
The notation
\mbox{diag}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})
defines a
k\times k
diagonal matrix with the
j
-th diagonal element
a_{j}
for
j\in[k]
.
For a vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
v^{\otimes 2}
denotes the
n\times n
matrix
vv^{\top}
.
For two matrices
\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}
,
\bm{A}\preceq\bm{B}
means
\bm{B}-\bm{A}
is positive semi-definite. The class of all
m\times m
dimensional positive definite matrices is denoted by
\mathbb{S}^{m}_{+}
. The notation
\mu_{n}\xrightarrow{w}\mu
means that the sequence of measures
\mu_{n}
converges weakly to
\mu
. Finally, let
B_{p}(r)
denote the set
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\|x\|\leq r\}
.

2
Methods

Suppose on
N
subjects, we observe multimodal data
\{\bm{X}_{h}:h\in[m]\}
and
\{\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\ell}:\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]\}
as generated in (
1.1
)–(
1.2
).
In this section, we first introduce an algorithm for the integrative estimation of the signal components in these data matrices.
In addition, we propose a method for

constructing prediction set of latent state encodings

across all modalities for a new subject (i.e., a query) on which only a subset of modalities/features are observed.

2.1
Integrative signal recovery with OrchAMP

2.1.1
An initial singular value decomposition

To facilitate the description of our method, we rescale the high-dimensional matrices by
\sqrt{N}

to define

\displaystyle\widebar{\bm{X}}_{h}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\bm{X}_{h},\quad h\in[m].

(2.1)

Recall that the
signal
in

\widebar{\bm{X}}_{h}
is of rank
r_{h}
.
We write the best rank
r_{h}
approximation to
\widebar{\bm{X}}_{h}
as

\frac{1}{N}{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}\bm{D}_{0,h}({\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})^{\top}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{r_{h}}(\bm{D}_{0,h})_{kk}\;({\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})_{*k}\;({\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})^{\top}_{*k},

(2.2)

where
\bm{D}_{0,h}
is the diagonal matrix consisting of the top
r_{h}

singular values of

\widebar{\bm{X}}_{h}
and
{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}
and
{\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}
are the matrices of the corresponding sample left and right singular vectors rescaled such that

({\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})^{\top}{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}=N\,\bm{I}_{r_{h}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad({\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})^{\top}{\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}=p_{h}\,\bm{I}_{r_{h}}.

(2.3)

Further, we assume that for all
h\in[m]
and
j\in[r_{h}]

\displaystyle({\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})^{\top}_{*j}(\bm{U}_{h})_{*j}\geq 0,\quad\mbox{and}\quad({\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})^{\top}_{*j}(\bm{V}_{h})_{*j}\geq 0.

(2.4)

This assumption is taken to enforce identifiability of the latent factors since
otherwise they
are only identifiable up to columnwise sign flips.

When the population singular values
\{(\bm{D}_{h})_{kk}:k\in[r_{h}]\}
are sufficiently large
1
1

1

To be exact, when each
(\bm{D}_{h})_{kk}>\gamma_{h}^{-1/4}

as

N,p_{h}\to\infty
with
p_{h}/N\to\gamma_{h}\in(0,\infty)
.
This condition
corresponds to a Baik–Ben Arous–Péché (BBP) type threshold
[
2
]
that ensures separation of the signal components from the bulk of the spectrum.
, one can show that
2
2

2

For the precise formulation of the approximation in (
2.5
), see Proposition
5.1
.
,

\displaystyle{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}\approx\bm{U}_{h}(\bm{S}^{L,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})^{\top}+\bm{Z}^{L}_{h}(\bm{\Sigma}^{L,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})^{1/2},\hskip 15.00002pt{\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}\approx\bm{V}_{h}(\bm{S}^{R,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})^{\top}+\bm{Z}^{R}_{h}(\bm{\Sigma}^{R,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})^{1/2}.

(2.5)

Here
\bm{Z}^{L}_{h}
and
\bm{Z}^{R}_{h}
are independent white noise matrices with
(\bm{Z}^{L}_{h})_{ik},(\bm{Z}^{R}_{h})_{jk}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}N(0,1)
for

i\in[N],j\in[p_{h}]
, and
k\in[r_{h}]
.
In addition, for
\star\in\{L,R\}
,

{\bm{S}}^{\star,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}=\mathrm{diag}((s^{\star}_{0,h})_{1},\dots,(s^{\star}_{0,h})_{r_{h}}),\quad{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\star,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}=\mathrm{diag}((\sigma^{\star}_{0,h})^{2}_{1},\dots,(\sigma^{\star}_{0,h})_{r_{h}}^{2}),

(2.6)

where
for all
h\in[m]
and
k\in[r_{h}]
,

\displaystyle(\sigma^{L}_{0,h})^{2}_{k}=\frac{1+(\bm{D}_{h})^{2}_{kk}}{(\bm{D}_{h})^{2}_{kk}\{\gamma_{h}\,(\bm{D}_{h})^{2}_{kk}+1\}},

\displaystyle(\sigma^{R}_{0,h})^{2}_{k}=\frac{1+\gamma_{h}\,(\bm{D}_{h})^{2}_{kk}}{\gamma_{h}\,(\bm{D}_{h})^{2}_{kk}\{(\bm{D}_{h})^{2}_{kk}+1\}},

(2.7)

\displaystyle(s^{L}_{0,h})_{k}=\sqrt{1-(\sigma^{L}_{0,h})^{2}_{k}},

\displaystyle(s^{R}_{0,h})_{k}=\sqrt{1-(\sigma^{R}_{0,h})^{2}_{k}}.

The representations (
2.5
)–(
2.7
) and the model (
1.1
)–(
1.2
)
enable one to infer the population singular vectors in
\bm{U}_{h}
and
\bm{V}_{h}
based on their sample counterparts and
\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{\ell}
based on the leading left singular vectors
\widetilde{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,\ell}
of
\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\ell}
within each dataset.
However,
when
there is dependence among these signals, it is necessary to integrate information across all datasets for statistical efficiency even if one is only interested in the signal within a single
modality
.

2.1.2
Estimation of nuisance parameters

Under appropriate assumptions,
we can consistently estimate the signal strengths
(\bm{D}_{h})_{kk}
for all
h\in[m]
and
k\in[r_{h}]
by the square roots of

\displaystyle(\widehat{\bm{D}}_{h})^{2}_{kk}=\frac{\gamma_{h}(\bm{D}_{0,h})^{2}_{kk}-(1+\gamma_{h})+\sqrt{[\gamma_{h}(\bm{D}_{0,h})^{2}_{kk}-(1+\gamma_{h})]^{2}-4\gamma_{h}}}{2\gamma_{h}}.

(2.8)

Consequently, we can construct consistent estimators of
{\bm{S}}^{\star,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h},{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\star,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}
for
\star\in\{L,R\}
and
h\in[m]
as

\widehat{\bm{S}}^{\star,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}=\mathrm{diag}((\widehat{s}^{\star}_{0,h})_{1},\dots,(\widehat{s}^{\star}_{0,h})_{r_{h}}),\quad\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\star,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}=\mathrm{diag}((\widehat{\sigma}^{\star}_{0,h})^{2}_{1},\dots,(\widehat{\sigma}^{\star}_{0,h})_{r_{h}}^{2}),

(2.9)

where for
k\in[r_{h}]
,
(\widehat{s}^{\star}_{0,h})_{k}
and
(\widehat{\sigma}^{\star}_{0,h})_{k}
are obtained from replacing
({\bm{D}}_{h})_{kk}
with
(\widehat{\bm{D}}_{h})_{kk}
in (
2.7
).
For low-dimensional modalities, we can consistently estimate the matrices
\bm{L}_{\ell}
for
\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]
by

\widehat{\bm{L}}_{\ell}=\Big(\frac{1}{N}\widetilde{\bm{X}}^{\top}_{\ell}\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\ell}-\bm{I}_{{\widetilde{r}}_{\ell}}\Big)^{1/2}.

(2.10)

2.1.3
Iterative refinement via OrchAMP

To incorporate information across multiple modalities,
we design an iterative refinement algorithm for
\{{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h},{\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}:h\in[m]\}
, and
\{\widetilde{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,\ell}:\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]\}
,
which generates a sequence of estimates

\{\bm{U}_{t,h},\bm{V}_{t,h}:h\in[m]\}
and
\{\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{t,\ell}:\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]\}
for
t\geq 0
, and information integration across all datasets occurs at each iteration.

In the ideal situation, the optimal updates in each iteration require knowledge of the priors
\mu
and
\{\nu_{h}:h\in[m]\}
.
In practice, this knowledge is unavailable and we adopt the empirical Bayes approach in
[
59
]
to obtain data-based estimators of these priors.

Estimating priors

Let
r=\sum_{h=1}^{m}r_{h}
and
\widetilde{r}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\widetilde{m}}\widetilde{r}_{\ell}
.
The approximation in (
2.5
) suggests that
the corresponding rows in the matrices
\{\bm{U}_{0,h}:h\in[m]\}
and

\{\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\ell}:\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]\}

can be viewed as i.i.d. samples from the joint distribution of

(Y_{0,1},\ldots,Y_{0,m},\widetilde{Y}_{0,1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{0,\widetilde{m}})\in\mathbb{R}^{r+\widetilde{r}}

where conditional on
(U_{1},\ldots,U_{m},\tilde{U}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{U}_{\widetilde{m}})
,

\displaystyle Y_{0,h}\sim N_{r_{h}}(\bm{S}^{L,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}U_{h},\bm{\Sigma}^{L,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}),\,h\in[m],\quad\widetilde{Y}_{0,\ell}\sim N_{\widetilde{r}_{\ell}}(\bm{L}_{\ell}\widetilde{U}_{\ell},\bm{I}_{{\widetilde{r}}_{\ell}}),\,\ell\in[\widetilde{m}].

(2.11)

Hence, the (approximate) likelihood of
\mu
given
\{{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}:h\in[m]\}
and

\{\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\ell}:\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]\}

is

\displaystyle L(\mu;\{\bm{S}^{L,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h},\bm{\Sigma}^{L,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}:h\in[m]\},\{{\bm{L}}_{\ell}:\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]\})

(2.12)

\displaystyle\propto~~\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Bigg[\int\prod_{h=1}^{m}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}[({\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})_{i*}-\bm{S}^{L,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}(\bm{U}_{h})_{i*}]^{\top}(\bm{\Sigma}^{L,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})^{-1}[({\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})_{i*}-\bm{S}^{L,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}(\bm{U}_{h})_{i*}]\right)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\ell=1}^{\widetilde{m}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}[(\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\ell})_{i*}-\bm{L}_{\ell}(\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{\ell})_{i*}]^{\top}[(\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\ell})_{i*}-\bm{L}_{\ell}(\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{\ell})_{i*}]\right)

\displaystyle\times d\mu((\bm{U}_{1})_{i*},\ldots,(\bm{U}_{m})_{i*},(\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{1})_{i*},\ldots,(\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{\widetilde{m}})_{i*})\Bigg].

When the prior
\mu
belongs to
some class of prior distributions
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathbb{R}^{r+\widetilde{r}}
, we define its approximate MLE as

\widehat{\mu}=\argmax_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}}L(\mu;\{\widehat{\bm{S}}^{L,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{L,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}:h\in[m]\},\{\widehat{\bm{L}}_{\ell}:\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]\}).

(2.13)

Note that in (
2.13
), we have replaced
\{\bm{S}^{L,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h},\bm{\Sigma}^{L,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}:h\in[m]\}
and
\{{\bm{L}}_{\ell}:\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]\}
in the definition of
L
with their sample counterparts in (
2.9
) and (
2.10
), and hence
\widehat{\mu}
is fully data-driven.

Similarly, the rows of
\bm{V}_{0,h}
can be regarded as iid samples from the model

Y^{R}_{0,h}\,\big|\,V_{h}\sim N_{r_{h}}(\bm{S}^{R,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}V_{h},\bm{\Sigma}^{R,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})\quad\mbox{with}\quad V_{h}\sim\nu_{h}\quad\mbox{for $h\in[m]$.}

(2.14)

As the
V_{h}
’s are mutually independent,
for each
h\in[m]
,
the approximate MLE of
\nu_{h}
is given by

\displaystyle\widehat{\nu}_{h}

\displaystyle=\argmax_{\nu_{h}\in\mathcal{P}_{\nu_{h}}}\prod_{i=1}^{p_{h}}\Bigg[\int\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}[({\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})_{i*}-\widehat{\bm{S}}^{R,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}(\bm{V}_{h})_{i*}]^{\top}(\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{R,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})^{-1}[({\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})_{i*}-\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 200.0003pt\left.\widehat{\bm{S}}^{R,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}(\bm{V}_{h})_{i*}]\right)d\nu_{h}((\bm{V}_{h})_{i*})\Bigg],

(2.15)

where
\mathcal{P}_{\nu_{h}}
is a class of priors on
\mathbb{R}^{r_{h}}
.

We defer
examples
of possible classes of priors
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\nu_{h}}
to Appendix J.1-J.2.

Updating estimators for the latent factors

To initialize the iterative updates, we define
\bm{V}_{0,h}={\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}
and
\widebar{\bm{U}}_{-1,h}={\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}(\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{R}_{0,h})^{1/2}
for
h\in[m]
where
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{R}_{0,h}=\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{R,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}
for
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{R,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}
defined in (
2.9
). Additionally, we set
\widehat{\bm{S}}^{R}_{0,h}=\widehat{\bm{S}}^{R,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}
for all
h\in[m]
.
The proposed OrchAMP updates
\{\widebar{\bm{U}}_{t,h},\widebar{\bm{V}}_{t,h}:h\in[m]\}
for estimating

\{{\bm{U}}_{h},{\bm{V}}_{h}:h\in[m]\}

are defined recursively for all integer
t\geq 0
as:

\displaystyle\widebar{\bm{V}}_{t,h}

\displaystyle=v_{t,h}(\bm{V}_{t,h};\widehat{\nu}_{h}),

(2.16)

\displaystyle{\bm{U}}_{t,h}

\displaystyle=\widebar{\bm{X}}_{h}\widebar{\bm{V}}_{t,h}-\gamma_{h}\widebar{\bm{U}}_{t-1,h}(\bm{J}^{R}_{t,h}\big(\bm{V}_{t,h};\widehat{\nu}_{h})\big)^{\top},

\displaystyle\widebar{\bm{U}}_{t,h}

\displaystyle=u_{t,h}(\bm{U}_{t,1},\ldots,\bm{U}_{t,m},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\widetilde{m}};\widehat{\mu}),

\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt\bm{V}_{t+1,h}

\displaystyle=\widebar{\bm{X}}^{\top}_{h}\widebar{\bm{U}}_{t,h}-\widebar{\bm{V}}_{t,h}(\bm{J}^{L}_{t,h}\big(\bm{U}_{t,1},\ldots,\bm{U}_{t,m},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\widetilde{m}};\widehat{\mu})\big)^{\top}.

where the

denoisers

v_{t,h}:\mathbb{R}^{r_{h}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{r_{h}}
and
u_{t,h}:\mathbb{R}^{r+{\widetilde{r}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{r_{h}}

are

\displaystyle v_{t,h}(x;\widehat{\nu}_{h})=\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\nu}_{h}}\left[V_{h}\,\Big|\,\widehat{\bm{S}}^{R}_{t,h}V_{h}+(\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{R}_{t,h})^{1/2}Z_{h}=x\right],

(2.17)

\displaystyle u_{t,h}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},\widetilde{x}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{x}_{\widetilde{m}};\widehat{\mu})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mu}}\left[U_{h}\,\Big|\,\widehat{Y}_{t,1}=x_{1},\ldots,\widehat{Y}_{t,m}=x_{m},\widetilde{Y}_{1}=\widetilde{x}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{m}}=\widetilde{x}_{\widetilde{m}}\right].

In (
2.17
),

Z_{h}\sim N_{r_{h}}(0,{\bm{I}}_{r_{h}})
,
V_{h}\sim\widehat{\nu}_{h}
and
V_{h}
,
Z_{h}
, and
(\widehat{\bm{S}}^{R}_{t,h},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{R}_{t,h})
are mutually independent.
In addition,

\widehat{Y}_{t,h}\,\big|\,U_{h}\sim N_{r_{h}}(\widehat{\bm{S}}^{L}_{t,h}U_{h},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{L}_{t,h}),\;\widetilde{Y}_{\ell}\,\big|\,\widetilde{U}_{\ell}\sim N_{\widetilde{r}_{\ell}}(\widehat{\bm{L}}_{\ell}\widetilde{U}_{\ell},\bm{I}_{\widetilde{r}_{\ell}}),\;(U_{1},\ldots,U_{m},\tilde{U}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{U}_{\widetilde{m}})\sim\widehat{\mu}

and are independent of
\{\widehat{\bm{S}}^{L}_{t,h},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{L}_{t,h}:h\in[m]\}
and
\{\widehat{\bm{L}}_{\ell}:\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]\}
. Furthermore, conditional on
\{\widehat{\bm{S}}^{L}_{t,h},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{L}_{t,h}:h\in[m]\}
,
\{\widehat{\bm{L}}_{\ell}:\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]\}
,
(U_{1},\ldots,U_{m},\tilde{U}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{U}_{\widetilde{m}})
, elements in the collection
\{\widehat{Y}_{t,1},\dots,\widehat{Y}_{t,m},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{m}}\}
are mutually independent.
The matrices
\{\widehat{\bm{S}}^{L}_{t,h},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{L}_{t,h},\widehat{\bm{S}}^{R}_{t,h},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{R}_{t,h}:t\geq 0\}
are
state evolution parameters
and are defined by the following state evolution recursions:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{L}_{t,h}=\frac{1}{N}(\widebar{\bm{V}}_{t,h})^{\top}\widebar{\bm{V}}_{t,h},

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{R}_{t+1,h}=\frac{1}{N}(\widebar{\bm{U}}_{t,h})^{\top}\widebar{\bm{U}}_{t,h},

(2.18)

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{S}}^{L}_{t,h}=\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{L}_{t,h}\widehat{\bm{D}}_{h},

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{S}}^{R}_{t+1,h}=\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{R}_{t+1,h}\widehat{\bm{D}}_{h}.

With (
2.17
), the matrices
v_{t,h}(\bm{V}_{t,h};\widehat{\nu}_{h})\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{h}\times r_{h}}
and
u_{t,h}(\bm{U}_{t,1},\ldots,\bm{U}_{t,m},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\widetilde{m}};\widehat{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times r_{h}}
are defined by applying the denoisers in a rowwise fashion to the matrix arguments:

\displaystyle v_{t,h}(\bm{V}_{t,h};\widehat{\nu}_{h})_{k*}=v_{t,h}((\bm{V}_{t,h})_{k*};\widehat{\nu}_{h}),

(2.19)

\displaystyle u_{t,h}(\bm{U}_{t,1},\ldots,\bm{U}_{t,m},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\widetilde{m}};\widehat{\mu})_{k*}

\displaystyle=u_{t,h}((\bm{U}_{t,1})_{k*},\ldots,(\bm{U}_{t,m})_{k*},(\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1})_{k*},\ldots,(\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\widetilde{m}})_{k*};\widehat{\mu}).

Furthermore, the matrix
\bm{J}^{R}_{t,h}(\bm{V}_{t,h};\widehat{\nu}_{h})\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{h}\times r_{h}}
is defined entrywise as follows:

\left[\bm{J}^{R}_{t,h}(\bm{V}_{t,h};\widehat{\nu}_{h})\right]_{ij}=\frac{1}{p_{h}}\sum_{k=1}^{p_{h}}\frac{\partial v_{t,h,i}}{\partial x_{j}}\left((\bm{V}_{t,h})_{k*};\widehat{\nu}_{h}\right),\quad(i,j)\in[r_{h}]\times[r_{h}],

where
v_{t,h}(\cdot)=(v_{t,h,1}(\cdot),\ldots,v_{t,h,r_{h}}(\cdot))^{\top}
and
\partial v_{t,h,i}/\partial x_{j}
is the partial derivative of
v_{t,h,i}
with respect to its
j
-th argument.
Next, for
h\in[m]
, let

\mathcal{I}_{h}=\left\{\sum_{k=1}^{h-1}r_{k}+1,\ldots,\sum_{k=1}^{h}r_{k}\right\}

(2.20)

and for
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},\widetilde{x}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{x}_{\widetilde{m}})\in\mathbb{R}^{r+\widetilde{r}}
,
let
\bm{J}_{t,h}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},\widetilde{x}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{x}_{\widetilde{m}};\widehat{\mu})

be the Jacobian matrix of the function

u_{t,h}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},\widetilde{x}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{x}_{\widetilde{m}};\widehat{\mu})
and

\left[\bm{J}_{t,h}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},\widetilde{x}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{x}_{\widetilde{m}};\widehat{\mu})\right]_{*\mathcal{I}_{h}}\in\mathbb{R}^{(r+\widetilde{r})\times r_{h}}

be the submatrix of
\bm{J}_{t,h}
that contains only the columns in
\mathcal{I}_{h}
.
Then, in the last line of (
2.16
), the quantity

\displaystyle\bm{J}^{L}_{t,h}(\bm{U}_{t,1},\ldots,\bm{U}_{t,m},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\widetilde{m}};\widehat{\mu})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left[\bm{J}_{t,h}((\bm{U}_{t,1})_{k*},\ldots,(\bm{U}_{t,m})_{k*},(\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1})_{k*},\ldots,(\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\widetilde{m}})_{k*};\widehat{\mu})\right]_{*\mathcal{I}_{h}}.

In summary, the OrchAMP updates in (
2.16
) can be roughly decomposed into three parts:

1.

Computing
\widebar{\bm{X}}_{h}\widebar{\bm{V}}_{t,h}
and
\widebar{\bm{X}}_{h}^{\top}\widebar{\bm{U}}_{t,h}
is
a
power iteration for improving estimation accuracy of left and right singular vectors, respectively.

2.

The subtraction of
\widebar{\bm{V}}_{t,h}(\bm{J}^{L}_{t,h})^{\top}
from
\bm{X}^{\top}_{h}\widebar{\bm{U}}_{t,h}
and of
\gamma_{h}\widebar{\bm{U}}_{t-1,h}(\bm{J}^{R}_{t,h}(\bm{V}_{t,h};\widehat{\nu}_{h}))^{\top}
from
\bm{X}_{h}\widebar{\bm{V}}_{t,h}
removes the dependence on past iterates from the current, which enables tracking of the asymptotic distributions of
\{\widebar{\bm{U}}_{t,h},\widebar{\bm{V}}_{t,h}:h\in[m]\}
.

3.

The matrices
\bm{U}_{t,h}
and
\bm{V}_{t,h}
are intermediate estimates of
\bm{U}_{h}
and
\bm{V}_{h}
in the
t
-th iteration.
They are further improved by the denoisers
u_{t,h}
and
v_{t,h}
, respectively, to construct the final estimates
\widebar{\bm{U}}_{t,h}
and
\widebar{\bm{V}}_{t,h}
in the
t
-th iteration.

Updating estimators for
\{\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{\ell}:\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]\}

For each fixed
\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]
, define
the denoiser at the
t
-th iteration

\widetilde{u}_{t,\ell}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},\widetilde{x}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{x}_{\widetilde{m}};\widehat{\mu}):\mathbb{R}^{r+\widetilde{r}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{{\widetilde{r}}_{\ell}}

as

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{t,\ell}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},\widetilde{x}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{x}_{\widetilde{m}};\widehat{\mu})

(2.21)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mu}}\left[\widetilde{U}_{\ell}\,\Big|\,\widehat{Y}_{t,1}=x_{1},\ldots,\widehat{Y}_{t,m}=x_{m},\widetilde{Y}_{1}=\widetilde{x}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{m}}=\widetilde{x}_{\widetilde{m}}\right],

where the joint distribution of
\{\widehat{Y}_{t,1},\dots,\widehat{Y}_{t,m},\widetilde{Y}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{Y}_{\widetilde{m}}\}
is the same as that in (
2.17
).

For
\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]
and
t\geq 0
, we propose the following denoised estimates for the subject effects of the low-dimensional modalities:

\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{t,\ell}=\widetilde{u}_{t,\ell}(\bm{U}_{t,1},\ldots,\bm{U}_{t,m},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\widetilde{m}};\widehat{\mu}).

(2.22)

The matrices,
\widetilde{u}_{t,\ell}(\bm{U}_{t,1},\ldots,\bm{U}_{t,m},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\widetilde{m}};\widehat{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times\widetilde{r}_{\ell}}
satisfies

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{t,\ell}(\bm{U}_{t,1},\ldots,\bm{U}_{t,m},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\widetilde{m}};\widehat{\mu})_{k*}

(2.23)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{u}_{t,\ell}((\bm{U}_{t,1})_{k*},\ldots,(\bm{U}_{t,m})_{k*},(\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1})_{k*},\ldots,(\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\widetilde{m}})_{k*};\widehat{\mu}),\quad k\in[N].

2.1.4
Summary

All details of the integrative signal recovery procedure are summarized in

Algorithm
1
.

Algorithm 1

Orchestrated Approximate Message Passing

1:

1.

Data matrices
\widebar{\bm{X}}_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times p_{h}}
for
h\in[m]
and
\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times{\widetilde{r}}_{\ell}}
for
\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]
;

2.

Signal ranks of high-dimensional modalities
\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{m}\}
;

3.

Maximum number of iterates
T
.

2:

Initialization:

1.

Perform SVD on each
\widebar{\bm{X}}_{h}
to get its best rank
r_{h}
approximation
\frac{1}{N}{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}\bm{D}_{0,h}({\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h})^{\top}
for
h\in[m]
.

2.

Compute
\widehat{\bm{D}}_{h}
,
\widehat{\bm{S}}^{L,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{L,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h},\widehat{\bm{S}}^{R,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{R,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}
for
h\in[m]
as in (
2.8
)-(
2.9
) and
\widehat{\bm{L}}_{\ell}
for
\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]
as in (
2.10
).

3.

Compute empirical Bayes priors
\widehat{\mu}
and
\widehat{\nu}_{h}
for
h\in[m]
as in (
2.13
) and (
2.15
).

4.

Set
\widehat{\bm{S}}^{R}_{0,h}\leftarrow\widehat{\bm{S}}^{R,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}
,
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{R}_{0,h}\leftarrow\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{R,\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}
,
\bm{V}_{0,h}={\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}
, and
\widebar{\bm{U}}_{-1,h}={\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}(\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{R}_{0,h})^{1/2}
for
h\in[m]
.

3:

for

t=0,\ldots,T

do

\triangleright
Iterative refinement

4:

for

h=1,\ldots,m

do

5:

\widebar{\bm{V}}_{t,h}\leftarrow v_{t,h}(\bm{V}_{t,h};\widehat{\nu}_{h})
;

\triangleright
Update the estimate of
\bm{V}_{h}

6:

\bm{U}_{t,h}\leftarrow\widebar{\bm{X}}_{h}\widebar{\bm{V}}_{t,h}-\gamma_{h}\widebar{\bm{U}}_{t-1,h}(\bm{J}^{R}_{t,h}(\bm{V}_{t,h};\widehat{\nu}_{h}))^{\top}
;

7:

\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{L}_{t,h}\leftarrow\frac{1}{N}(\widebar{\bm{V}}_{t,h})^{\top}\widebar{\bm{V}}_{t,h}

and

\widehat{\bm{S}}^{L}_{t,h}\leftarrow\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{L}_{t,h}\widehat{\bm{D}}_{h}
;

8:

\widebar{\bm{U}}_{t,h}\leftarrow u_{t,h}(\bm{U}_{t,1},\ldots,\bm{U}_{t,m},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\widetilde{m}};\widehat{\mu})
;

\triangleright
Update the estimate of
\bm{U}_{h}
via integration

9:

\bm{V}_{t+1,h}\leftarrow\widebar{\bm{X}}^{\top}_{h}\widebar{\bm{U}}_{t,h}-\widebar{\bm{V}}_{t,h}(\bm{J}^{L}_{t,h}(\bm{U}_{t,1},\ldots,\bm{U}_{t,m},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\widetilde{m}};\widehat{\mu}))^{\top}
;

10:

\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{R}_{t+1,h}\leftarrow\frac{1}{N}(\widebar{\bm{U}}_{t,h})^{\top}\widebar{\bm{U}}_{t,h}

and

\widehat{\bm{S}}^{R}_{t+1,h}\leftarrow\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{R}_{t+1,h}\widehat{\bm{D}}_{h}
.

11:

for

\ell=1,\ldots,\widetilde{m}

do

12:

\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{t,\ell}\leftarrow\widetilde{u}_{t,\ell}(\bm{U}_{t,1},\ldots,\bm{U}_{t,m},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\widetilde{m}};\widehat{\mu})
.

\triangleright
Update the estimate of
\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{\ell}
via integration

13:

\{\widebar{\bm{U}}_{T,1},\ldots,\widebar{\bm{U}}_{T,m},\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{T,1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{T,\widetilde{m}}\}
and

\{\widebar{\bm{V}}_{T,1},\ldots,\widebar{\bm{V}}_{T,m}\}
.

Remark 2.1
.

When different modalities have different signal ranks,
there exists modality-specific information and such information must not be destroyed in the process of integration.
Since our algorithm can handle different signal ranks for different modalities, it helps incorporating modality-specific information in the construction of the refined estimators
\widebar{\bm{U}}_{t,h}
,
\widebar{\bm{V}}_{t,h}
, and
\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{t,\ell}
.

2.2
Latent representation prediction set of query data

2.2.1
Problem formulation

We now consider predicting the latent representation of a query subject with partially observed data about it:
for a query subject, we observe
d\in[m]
high-dimensional modalities and
\widetilde{d}\in[\widetilde{m}]
low-dimensional modalities, and for each observed modality, potentially only a subset of modality-specific features are observed.
Our goal is to construct a prediction set for the realized value of the query’s latent factor representation
({U}_{1}^{\mathrm{Q}},\dots,{U}_{m}^{\mathrm{Q}},\widetilde{U}_{1}^{\mathrm{Q}},\dots,\widetilde{U}_{\widetilde{m}}^{\mathrm{Q}})
by leveraging the availability of
\{\bm{X}_{1},\dots,\bm{X}_{m},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{\widetilde{m}}\}
that will be referred to as the reference data in the following discussion.

To be precise, we observe
\{Q_{h_{1}},\ldots,Q_{h_{d}},\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}}\}
on a query subject, where
Q_{h_{1}},\ldots,Q_{h_{d}}
are from high-dimensional modalities
\{h_{1},\ldots,h_{d}\}\subseteq[m]
and
\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}}
low-dimensional modalities
\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{\widetilde{d}}\}\subseteq[\widetilde{m}]
.
For the
k
-th observed high-dimensional modality,

Q_{h_{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{F}_{h_{k}}|}
can be written as

Q_{h_{k}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}(\bm{V}_{h_{k}})_{\mathcal{F}_{h_{k}}*}\bm{D}_{h_{k}}U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{k}}+W^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{k}},\quad\mbox{for $W^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{k}}\sim N_{|\mathcal{F}_{h_{k}}|}(0,\bm{I}_{|\mathcal{F}_{h_{k}}|})$.}

(2.24)

In (
2.24
),
\mathcal{F}_{h_{k}}\subseteq[p_{h_{k}}]
collects indices of the observed features and
U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{h_{k}}}
denotes the query’s realized subject effect in the modality.
We assume that
the matrices
\bm{D}_{h_{k}}
and
\bm{V}_{h_{k}}
are shared by the query data
Q_{h_{k}}
and the reference data
\bm{X}_{h_{k}}
, for all
k\in[d]
,
and that
the noise vectors
\{W^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{k}}:k\in[d]\}
are mutually independent and are independent of the reference data.
Furthermore, we assume that the number of observed features is comparable to the total number of features in reference data for any observed high-dimensional modality in the query:
for all
k\in[d]
,
{|\mathcal{F}_{h_{k}}|}/{p_{h_{k}}}\rightarrow\lambda_{h_{k}}\in(0,1]
, as
p_{h_{k}}\rightarrow\infty
.
For the
k
-th observed low-dimensional modality,

\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell_{k}}|}
can be written as

\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{k}}=(\bm{L}_{\ell_{k}})_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell_{k}}*}\widetilde{U}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{k}}+\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{k}},\quad\mbox{for $\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{k}}\sim N_{|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell_{k}}|}(0,\bm{I}_{|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell_{k}}|})$.}

(2.25)

In (
2.25
),
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell_{k}}\subseteq[\widetilde{r}_{\ell_{k}}]
denotes indices of the observed features in this modality,

{\widetilde{U}}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\widetilde{r}_{\ell_{k}}}
represents the query’s realized subject effect in the modality, and the noise vector
\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{k}}
is independent of that in other modalities and of those in reference data.
Finally, we assume that

(U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{1}},\dots,U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{d}},\widetilde{U}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{1}},\dots,\widetilde{U}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}})\sim\mu_{h_{1},\ldots,h_{d};\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{\widetilde{d}}},

where
\mu_{h_{1},\ldots,h_{d};\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{\widetilde{d}}}
is the marginal distribution of
(U_{h_{1}},\ldots,U_{h_{d}},\widetilde{U}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{U}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}})
when
(U_{1},\ldots,U_{m},\widetilde{U}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{U}_{\widetilde{m}})\sim\mu
that is the data generating prior for the reference datasets.

2.2.2
Prediction set construction

We are to construct a prediction set in the form of an
\ell_{2}
ball.
We start with point prediction of the latent subject effect vector of the query that serves as the center of the prediction set.
For the
k
-th observed high-dimensional modality in the query, we first estimate
(\bm{V}_{h_{k}})_{\mathcal{F}_{h_{k}}*}
by
(\widebar{\bm{V}}_{T,h_{k}})_{\mathcal{F}_{h_{k}}*}
where
\widebar{\bm{V}}_{T,h_{k}}
is the estimator of
{\bm{V}}_{h_{k}}
generated by Algorithm
1
.
The point prediction of the subject effect of the query in the
k
-th observed high-dimensional modality is then obtained by least squares as

\displaystyle\widehat{U}^{\mathrm{Q},0}_{h_{k}}:=(\widehat{\bm{R}}^{\top}_{h_{k}}\widehat{\bm{R}}_{h_{k}})^{-1}\widehat{\bm{R}}^{\top}_{h_{k}}\widebar{Q}_{h_{k}},

(2.26)

where
\widebar{Q}_{h_{k}}={1\over\sqrt{N}}Q_{h_{k}}
and for
\widehat{\bm{D}}_{h_{k}}
defined in (
2.8
),

\widehat{\bm{R}}_{h_{k}}:=\frac{1}{N}(\widebar{\bm{V}}_{T,h_{k}})_{\mathcal{F}_{h_{k}}*}\widehat{\bm{D}}_{h_{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{F}_{h_{k}}|\times r_{h_{k}}}
.

We shall later show that if
N
, the number of cells in the reference dataset, tends to infinity, then

\widehat{U}^{\mathrm{Q},0}_{h_{k}}=U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{k}}+\lambda^{-1/2}_{h_{k}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\widehat{\bm{D}}^{-1}_{h_{k}}(\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{L}_{T,h_{k}})^{-1}\widehat{\bm{D}}^{-1}_{h_{k}}\right)^{1/2}}Z_{h_{k}}+o_{p}(1),

(2.27)

where
Z_{h_{k}}\sim N_{r_{h_{k}}}(0,\bm{I}_{r_{h_{k}}})
and

\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{L}_{T,h_{k}}=\frac{1}{N}(\widebar{\bm{V}}_{T,h_{k}})^{\top}\widebar{\bm{V}}_{T,h_{k}}

.
To further integrate with other observed modalities,
let

r^{\mathrm{Q}}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}r_{h_{k}}
and
\widetilde{r}^{\mathrm{Q}}=\sum_{k=1}^{\widetilde{d}}|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell_{k}}|
.
In the same spirit as (
2.17
) and (
2.21
), we define denoisers
u^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h}:\mathbb{R}^{r^{\mathrm{Q}}+\widetilde{r}^{\mathrm{Q}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{r_{h}}
and
\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}:\mathbb{R}^{r^{\mathrm{Q}}+\widetilde{r}^{\mathrm{Q}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\widetilde{r}_{\ell}}
for
h\in[m]
,
\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]
, and
any

x=(x_{h_{1}},\ldots,x_{h_{d}},\widetilde{x}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{x}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}})\in\mathbb{R}^{r^{\mathrm{Q}}+\widetilde{r}^{\mathrm{Q}}}
as

\displaystyle u^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h}(x;\widehat{\mu})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mu}}\left[U_{h}\,\Big|\,Y^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{1}}=x_{h_{1}},\ldots,Y^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{d}}=x_{h_{d}},\widetilde{Y}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{1}}=\widetilde{x}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}}=\widetilde{x}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}}\right],

(2.28)

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}(x;\widehat{\mu})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\mu}}\left[\widetilde{U}_{\ell}\,\Big|\,Y^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{1}}=x_{h_{1}},\ldots,Y^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{d}}=x_{h_{d}},\widetilde{Y}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{1}}=\widetilde{x}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}}=\widetilde{x}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}}\right].

(2.29)

In (
2.28
)–(
2.29
), the sequence of priors
\widehat{\mu}
weakly converges to
\mu
as
N\rightarrow\infty
.
For
k\in[d]
and
\widetilde{k}\in[\widetilde{d}]
,

Y^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{k}}\sim N_{r_{h_{k}}}(U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{k}},\,\lambda^{-1}_{h_{k}}\widehat{\bm{D}}^{-1}_{h_{k}}(\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{L}_{T,h_{k}})^{-1}\widehat{\bm{D}}^{-1}_{h_{k}}),\quad\widetilde{Y}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{\widetilde{k}}}\sim N_{|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell_{\widetilde{k}}}|}((\widehat{\bm{L}}_{\ell_{\widetilde{k}}})_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell_{\widetilde{k}}}*}\widetilde{U}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{\widetilde{k}}},\,\bm{I}_{|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell_{\widetilde{k}}}|}),

(2.30)

where
(U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{1}},\ldots,U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{d}},\widetilde{U}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{U}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}})\sim\widehat{\mu}_{h_{1},\ldots,h_{d};\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{\widetilde{d}}}

with
\widehat{\mu}_{h_{1},\ldots,h_{d};\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{\widetilde{d}}}
being the marginal distribution of

(U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},\ldots,U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{m},\widetilde{U}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{U}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\widetilde{m}})\sim\widehat{\mu}
.
In addition, we assume that the random vectors
(U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{1}},\ldots,U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{d}},\widetilde{U}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{U}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}})
are independent of
\{\widehat{\bm{D}}_{h_{k}},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{L}_{T,h_{k}},\widehat{\bm{L}}_{\ell_{\widetilde{k}}}:k\in[d],\;\mbox{and}\;\widetilde{k}\in[\widetilde{d}]\}
and that the collection of variables

(Y^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{1}},\ldots,Y^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{d}},\widetilde{Y}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}})

are mutually independent given

(U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{1}},\ldots,U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{d}},\widetilde{U}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{U}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}}),

\{\widehat{\bm{D}}_{h_{k}},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{L}_{T,h_{k}}:k\in[d]\}
, and
\{\widehat{\bm{L}}_{\ell_{\widetilde{k}}}:\widetilde{k}\in[\widetilde{d}]\}
.
Finally, the point predictor for the subject effects of the query subject is given by:

\widehat{U}:=\big[(\widehat{U}^{H}_{1})^{\top}~\ldots~(\widehat{U}^{H}_{m})^{\top}~(\widehat{U}^{L}_{1})^{\top}~\ldots~(\widehat{U}^{L}_{\widetilde{m}})^{\top}\big]^{\top}

(2.31)

where, for all
h\in[m]
and
\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]
,

\widehat{U}^{H}_{h}=u^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h}((\widehat{U}^{\mathrm{Q},0}_{h_{1}},\ldots,\widehat{U}^{\mathrm{Q},0}_{h_{d}},\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}});\widehat{\mu})

and

\widehat{U}^{L}_{\ell}=\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}((\widehat{U}^{\mathrm{Q},0}_{h_{1}},\ldots,\widehat{U}^{\mathrm{Q},0}_{h_{d}},\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}});\widehat{\mu}).

Our
100\times(1-\alpha)\%
prediction set for the query’s latent representation vector is a ball centered at
\widehat{U}
.
To determine its radius, let
Y^{\mathrm{Q}}=(Y^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{1}},\ldots,Y^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h_{d}})\in\mathbb{R}^{r^{\mathrm{Q}}}
and
\widetilde{Y}^{\mathrm{Q}}=(Y^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{Y}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}})\in\mathbb{R}^{\widetilde{r}^{\mathrm{Q}}}
with components defined as in (
2.30
).
Now for

\displaystyle y^{\mathrm{Q}}=(\widehat{U}^{\mathrm{Q},0}_{h_{1}},\ldots,\widehat{U}^{\mathrm{Q},0}_{h_{d}})\in\mathbb{R}^{r^{\mathrm{Q}}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\widetilde{y}^{\mathrm{Q}}=(\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}})\in\mathbb{R}^{\widetilde{r}^{\mathrm{Q}}},

(2.32)

define the prediction ball radius
\widehat{b}_{\alpha}>0
for
\alpha\in(0,1)
as

\displaystyle\widehat{b}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\inf\bigg\{b>0:\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\mu}}\left[(U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},\ldots,U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{m},\widetilde{U}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{U}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\widetilde{m}})\in B_{r+\widetilde{r}}(\widehat{U},b)\,\Big|\,Y^{\mathrm{Q}}=y^{\mathrm{Q}},\widetilde{Y}^{\mathrm{Q}}=\widetilde{y}^{\mathrm{Q}}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 300.00046pt\geq 1-\alpha\bigg\},

(2.33)

where
(U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},\ldots,U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{m},\widetilde{U}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{U}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\widetilde{m}})\sim\widehat{\mu}
.
Thus, the
100\times(1-\alpha)\%
prediction set is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=B_{r+\widetilde{r}}(\widehat{U},\widehat{b}_{\alpha}).

(2.34)

The construction of the preceding prediction set is summarized in Algorithm
2
.

Remark 2.2
.

The radius
\widehat{b}_{\alpha}
can approximated by simulating a large number of samples from the conditional distribution described in (
2.33
) and using the
(1-\alpha)
-th sample quantile of the Euclidean distance between the sampled points and
\widehat{U}
.

Algorithm 2

Prediction Set of the subject effects for the Query Subjects

1:

1.

A query data entry with observed high-dimensional modalities
Q_{h_{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{F}_{h_{k}}|}
with observed features indexed by
\mathcal{F}_{h_{k}}
for
k\in[d]
and observed low-dimensional modalities
\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{l}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell_{l}}|}
with observed features indexed by
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell_{l}}
for
l\in[\widetilde{d}]
;

2.

Matrices
\{\widebar{\bm{V}}_{T,h_{1}},\ldots,\widebar{\bm{V}}_{T,h_{d}}\}
,
\{\widehat{\bm{D}}_{h}:h\in[m]\}
, and
\{\widehat{\bm{L}}_{\ell}:\ell\in[\widetilde{m}]\}
estimated from a reference dataset with
N
subjects according to Algorithm
1
, (
2.8
), and (
2.10
), respectively;

3.

A sequence of priors
\widehat{\mu}
converging to
\mu
weakly;

4.

Confidence level
100\times(1-\alpha)\%
.

2:

Compute
\widehat{\bm{R}}_{h_{k}}:=\frac{1}{N}(\widebar{\bm{V}}_{T,h_{k}})_{\mathcal{F}_{h_{k}}*}\widehat{\bm{D}}_{h_{k}}
for
k\in[d]
.

3:

Compute
\widehat{U}^{\mathrm{Q},0}_{h_{k}}:=(\widehat{\bm{R}}^{\top}_{h_{k}}\widehat{\bm{R}}_{h_{k}})^{-1}\widehat{\bm{R}}^{\top}_{h_{k}}\widebar{Q}_{h_{k}}
for
k\in[d]
, where
\widebar{Q}_{h_{k}}=Q_{h_{k}}/\sqrt{N}
.

4:

for

h=1,\ldots,m

do

5:

\widehat{U}^{H}_{h}\leftarrow u^{\mathrm{Q}}_{h}((\widehat{U}^{\mathrm{Q},0}_{h_{1}},\ldots,\widehat{U}^{\mathrm{Q},0}_{h_{d}},\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}});\widehat{\mu})
.

6:

for

\ell=1,\ldots,\widetilde{m}

do

7:

\widehat{U}^{L}_{\ell}\leftarrow\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}((\widehat{U}^{\mathrm{Q},0}_{h_{1}},\ldots,\widehat{U}^{\mathrm{Q},0}_{h_{d}},\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{Q}_{\ell_{\widetilde{d}}});\widehat{\mu})
.

8:

Construct
\widehat{U}
as in (
2.31
) and
\widehat{b}_{\alpha}
as in (
2.33
);

9:

The prediction set
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}=B_{r+\widetilde{r}}({\widehat{U}},{\widehat{b}_{\alpha}})
.

3
Numerical Experiments

In this section, we present numerical experiments on synthetic data to benchmark the performance of Algorithm
1
in signal recovery and the finite-sample coverage of the prediction set in Algorithm
2
.

3.1
Integrative signal recovery

We considered a simulation setting consisting of two high-dimensional modalities
\bm{X}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times p_{i}}
,
i=1,2
, and one low-dimensional modality
\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times\widetilde{r}}
, generated according to the models (
1.1
) and (
1.2
).
We had let
r_{1}=2
,
r_{2}=3
,

\widetilde{r}=2
,
p_{1}=\lfloor\gamma_{1}N\rfloor
and
p_{2}=\lfloor\gamma_{2}N\rfloor
for
N=10000
.
We fixed
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}=0.25
for this subsection.
Our primary interest here was to benchmark whether Algorithm
1
leverages the inter-modality dependence of signal components to achieve better signal recovery.

To this end, we let the rows of
\bm{U}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times 2}
be sampled as

(\bm{U}_{1})_{i*}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{3}}(T_{1i}+T_{2i})
,
where
T_{1i}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
and
T_{2i}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathrm{Unif}(\{-1,1\}^{2})
were mutually independent.

The first column of
\bm{U}_{2}
was generated independently by

(\bm{U}_{2})_{i,1}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathrm{Laplace}\left(0,\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)
.

The next two columns of
\bm{U}_{2}
were then generated via nonlinear transformations of the columns of
\bm{U}_{1}
.
Each entry in the second column of
{\bm{U}}_{2}
was generated as

(\bm{U}_{2})_{i2}=\rho\cdot\frac{\max(0,(\bm{U}_{1})_{i2})-\mathbb{E}[\max(0,(\bm{U}_{1})_{i2})]}{\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}[\max(0,(\bm{U}_{1})_{i2})]}}+\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}\cdot B_{i},

where
B_{i}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathrm{Rademacher}(\pm 1)
.
Each entry in the third column of
{\bm{U}}_{2}
was generated as

\displaystyle(\bm{U}_{2})_{i3}

\displaystyle=\rho\cdot\left\{\frac{\mathsf{h}((\bm{U}_{1})_{i1})-\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{h}((\bm{U}_{1})_{i1})]}{\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}[\mathsf{h}((\bm{U}_{1})_{i1})]}}\right\}+\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}\cdot Z_{i},

where
Z_{i}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}{N}(0,1)
and
\mathsf{h}(x):=\mathrm{sgn}(x)\min\{|x|,0.25\}
.
Moreover,
B_{i}
’s,
Z_{i}
’s and
\bm{U}_{1}
were mutually independent.

The signal
\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{1}
in
\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1}
was constructed as

(\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{1})_{i*}=\rho\cdot(\bm{U}_{1})_{i*}+\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}\cdot T_{3i},\quad T_{3i}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathrm{Unif}(\{-1,1\}^{2}),

where
T_{3i}
’s were independent of
\bm{U}_{1}
,
Z_{i}
’s, and
B_{i}
’s.

The loading matrices
\bm{V}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times r_{1}}
and
\bm{V}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{2}\times r_{2}}
were independently generated row-wise as:

(\bm{V}_{h})_{i*}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\sqrt{r_{h}}\cdot\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{r_{h}-1})
,
for

h\in\{1,2\}
.

The diagonal signal strength matrices in high-dimensional modalities were
defined by

\displaystyle(\bm{D}_{1})_{ii}=\lambda+3-i,\quad i=1,2;\qquad(\bm{D}_{2})_{ii}=2\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(4-i)},\quad i=1,\dots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}3}.

(3.1)

Finally, we set
\bm{L}_{1}=5\bm{I}_{2}+2.5\bm{1}_{2}\bm{1}_{2}^{\top}
in the low-dimensional modality.
In the foregoing definitions, the hyperparameter
\rho\in[0,1]
governs the level of inter-modality dependence of the signals, where larger
\rho
enforces stronger dependence.
The hyperparameter
\lambda>0
alters signal strength in the second high-dimensional modality.

We benchmarked the performance of OrchAMP against two others approaches:

•

EB-PCA
[
59
]
, which applies
denoising based on empirical priors, but separately
within
each modality without information sharing.

•

SVD, which applies neither denoising nor cross-modal integration.
Latent factors
\bm{U}_{h}
are estimated by
top left singular vectors
{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{0,h}
for
h\in\{1,2\}
, and

\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{1}
is estimated via least-squares projection
\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{1}\widehat{\bm{L}}_{1}(\widehat{\bm{L}}_{1}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{L}}_{1})^{-1}
, where
\widehat{\bm{L}}_{1}
is defined in (
2.10
).

All methods were applied to the triplet

(\bm{X}_{1},\bm{X}_{2},\widetilde{\bm{X}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}})
with the knowledge of the true signal ranks.
For any estimator triplet
(\widebar{\bm{U}}_{1},\widebar{\bm{U}}_{2},\widecheck{\bm{U}}_{1})
,
reconstruction accuracies were
quantified by the following normalized squared Frobenius loss:

\displaystyle\ell(\bm{U}_{h},\widebar{\bm{U}}_{h})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}r_{h}}\left\|\widebar{\bm{U}}_{h}\widebar{\bm{U}}_{h}^{\top}-\bm{U}_{h}\bm{U}_{h}^{\top}\right\|_{F}^{2},\,h\in\{1,2\};\,\ell(\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{1},\widecheck{\bm{U}}_{1})=\frac{1}{N^{2}\widetilde{r}}\left\|\widecheck{\bm{U}}_{1}\widecheck{\bm{U}}_{1}^{\top}-\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{1}\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{1}^{\top}\right\|_{F}^{2}.

The relative performance of the three methods are
expected to depend on both the signal strength
\lambda
and the strength of
inter-modality
dependence
\rho
,
and we varied these simulation hyperparameters
over the following grid to demonstrate such a dependence

\lambda\in\{2.0,2.4,2.8\},\quad\rho\in\{0.8,0.9,1.0\}.

In both OrchAMP and EB-PCA, we restricted each estimated prior to be a Gaussian mixture model (GMM) with four components and considered the results after
25
AMP iterations.

Table
1
reported average losses and the associated standard errors over
50
replicates for each
(\lambda,\rho)
pair.
By Table
1
, both OrchAMP and EB-PCA outperformed SVD across all
(\lambda,\rho)
configurations.
Moreover, the additional performance gain by OrchAMP was more pronounced for smaller values of
\lambda
, which is aligned with the intuition that borrowing strength from other modalities is most helpful for modalities with relatively low signal-to-noise ratios (SNRs).
Furthermore,
the performance of OrchAMP was not monotone in
\lambda
for the reconstruction of latent factors from Modality 2, since the difficulty in prior estimation is simultaneously affected by
\lambda
.
In summary, these experiments demonstrate that OrchAMP is particularly effective when the latent components of interest have weak SNRs and cannot be reliably recovered from individual modalities alone. In such cases, the use of information across the modalities allows for a more accurate recovery.
Within each modality, when the SNR increases, the performance of OrchAMP may be surpassed by that of EB-PCA as OrchAMP needs to estimate a higher-dimensional joint prior on latent factors, while EB-PCA only focuses on its marginal within each modality. This is the price that one pays for retaining the capacity of cross-modal querying, which EB-PCA does not offer.

3.2
Finite-sample

coverage

of prediction sets

Next we investigated the finite-sample coverage
of the prediction sets constructed via Algorithm
2
.
In this experiment, we considered two high-dimensional data matrices
\bm{X}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times p_{1}}
and
\bm{X}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times p_{2}}
generated according to the data generation scheme described in Section
3.1
(but without the low-dimensional modality), with ambient dimensions
p_{1}=p_{2}=\lfloor 0.25N\rfloor
.
We fixed
\rho=0.8
, and varied the sample size
N
and signal strength parameter
\lambda
as

N\in\{2000,3000,4000\},\quad\lambda\in\{5,7,9\}.

In each experiment, after obtaining the OrchAMP estimators on the training data, we queried them with
{\bm{X}}_{1}^{\mathrm{Q}}
(i.e., modality
1
) of an independent query sample generated from the same distribution.
In particular, we supplied
{\bm{X}}_{1}^{\mathrm{Q}}
and the OrchAMP estimators to Algorithm
2
to compute a
95\%
prediction set for the concatenated latent embedding
(U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{5}
and tested if it contained the true latent embedding of the query sample.
To estimate the radius
\widehat{b}_{\alpha}
of the prediction set, we simulated
10000
samples from the posterior distribution specified in (
2.33
) and took the
0.95
quantile of the corresponding Euclidean norms.
All other implementation details were identical to those used in Section
3.1
.

For each
(N,\lambda)
pair, we repeated the procedure described above over
50
independent replicates.
Table
2
reports the proportions of replicates
in which the true latent embedding
(U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},U^{\mathrm{Q}}_{2})
fell within the constructed prediction set.
Across all configuration, the coverage was close to the nominal
95\%
level, demonstrating that our prediction sets were reasonably well-calibrated even in finite samples.

It is possible that conformal prediction techniques could be adapted to the current context for achieving exact finite-sample calibration, and we leave this interesting problem for future research.

:

4
A
single-cell multi-omics
data example

In this section, we illustrate on a real data example the prowess of Algorithm
1
for constructing cell atlas with single-cell multi-omics data and of Algorithm
2
for probabilistic querying of the constructed atlas with uncertainty quantification where the query cell is only measured in one modality.

We focused on
a human peripheral blood mononuclear cell (PBMC) single-cell TEA-seq data

[
53
]
,

which

comprised three modalities: chromatin accessibility (
the
ATAC modality) information for the whole genome (around
66828
read pairs), gene expression levels (
the
RNA modality) for the whole genome (around
36601
transcripts), and abundance levels of
48
surface proteins (
the
Protein modality), for
8213
PBMC cells.
After pre-processing, we retained
a subset of
6323
cells with high data quality.
In addition, after feature screening, we selected
5000
highly variable read pairs for the ATAC modality,
2000
highly variable genes for the RNA modality,
and
40
highly variable proteins for the Protein modality
.
To conform with model (
1.1
)-(
1.2
), we applied the
\log 1p
transform
(x\mapsto\log(1+x))
entry-wise and subsequently centered each column (feature) in each modality
3
3

3

The
\log 1p
transformation maps the (total-counts-per-cell-normalized) discrete count measurements to a continuous scale and, by stabilizing the variance, mitigates the strong mean–variance dependence and skewness.
This step is standard in the pre-processing of single-cell RNA and ATAC data.
The post-transform variation around the latent signal is more homogeneous across features, making (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) more reasonable approximation to data.

.
Thus, the three modalities were represented by two high-dimensional matrices
\bm{X}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{6323\times 5000}
for ATAC and
\bm{X}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{6323\times 2000}
for RNA, and one low-dimensional matrix
\bm{\widetilde{X}}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{6323\times 40}
for Protein.

For each cell, we regarded its original cell type annotation in
[
53
]
as the ground-truth
for evaluation purpose
.

Neither Algorithm
1
or
2
had access to these annotations.

4.1
Cell atlas construction

Based on the comparison of the empirical singular value distribution with the square root of the (properly rescaled) Marchenko-Pastur distribution,
we set
r_{1}=15
and
r_{2}=20
.

See Fig
3
for scree plots of these two modalities.

We adopted the empirical Bayes technique for prior estimation with a Gaussian mixture class of priors (described in Appendix J.1) with 4 mixing components for estimating
\mu
, 11 mixing components to estimate
\nu_{1}
, and 6 mixing components to estimate
\nu_{2}
4
4

4

We selected the number of mixture components by Bayesian Information Criterion (BIC)
[
49
]
. Specifically, for the latent‐factor prior
\mu
we fit GMMs with
K\in\{2,\ldots,\lfloor\sqrt[3]{N}\rfloor\}
, and for the loading priors
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
we used
K\in\{2,\ldots,\lfloor\sqrt[3]{p_{1}}\rfloor\}
and
K\in\{2,\ldots,\lfloor\sqrt[3]{p_{2}}\rfloor\}
, respectively, retaining the
K
that minimized BIC for each prior.
.
We ran Algorithm
1
with
T=15
iterative updates and
learned
embeddings

\widebar{\bm{U}}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{6323\times 15}
,
\widebar{\bm{U}}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{6323\times 20}
, and
\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{6323\times 40}

for the ATAC, the RNA, and the Protein modalities, respectively.

Concatenating these embeddings for each cell, we got the following matrix of integrated multimodal embeddings as the constructed cell atlas:

\bm{U}=[\widebar{\bm{U}}_{1}\quad\widebar{\bm{U}}_{2}\quad\widetilde{\bm{U}}_{1}]\in\mathbb{R}^{6323\times 75}.

Figure
1
compares UMAP embeddings of the atlases built by Algorithm
1
(middle) with those by Seurat WNN
[
23
]
(left, with 50 PCs for ATAC and RNA and 40 PCs for Protein for optimized performance in metrics reported in Table
3
,
and those by
MOFA+
[
1
]
(right, with default tuning parameter choices).

There appears to be no substantial difference
across the three atlas UMAPs in terms of separating the cell types
.

Figure 1
:
UMAPs of PBMC atlases from
6323
TEA-seq cells by Seurat WNN (left), OrchAMP (middle), and MOFA+ (right),
colored according to original cell type annotations in
[
53
]
.

In addition to the qualitative visual inspection enabled by UMAPs,
we
report quantitative
metrics to further evaluate and compare the capacity of these atlases in delineating cell types.
Specifically, we compared these atlases
using four clustering metrics: the average silhouette score
[
46
]
, the V-measure
[
45
]
, the Adjusted Rand Index
[
24
]
, and the celltype Local Inverse Simpson’s Index (cLISI)
[
29
]
.
5
5

5

See Section
C
for further description of the clustering metrics.

These metrics were computed to compare the ground-truth cell type annotation with the cluster labels
of
the learned representations of all cells by
each method where the cluster labels were obtained from Louvain clustering
[
8
]
of the 20-nearest-neighbor graph constructed from the respective learned representations.
These
metrics collectively offer an objective assessment of how well clustering structure in the learned representations reflects ground-truth cell types: higher values of the average silhouette score, Adjusted Rand Index, and V-measure indicate stronger separation of major cell types in the resulting clusters, whereas lower cLISI values reflect improved clustering fidelity.
The metrics, summarized in Table
3
, provided additional evidence showing that OrchAMP achieves comparable performance in cell atlas construction to state-of-the-art methods while being theoretically justifiable and provides additional flexibility for downstream querying tasks.

See Section
B
for an additional multi-omics example.

.
